Exotic presentations of quaternion groups and Wall’s D2 problem
Tommy Hofmann, John Nicholson

Abstract
. The D2 problem of C. T. C. Wall asks whether every finite cohomologically 2-dimensional CW-complex is homotopy equivalent to a finite 2-complex. Several potential counterexamples have been proposed, the longest standing of which is a CW-complex constructed by Cohen and Dyer whose fundamental group is a quaternion group of order 32 32 . We show that this CW-complex is homotopy equivalent to the presentation 2-complex of a presentation constructed by Mannan–Popiel, thus showing it is not a counterexample to the D2 problem. We next introduce an infinite family of presentations for a quaternion group of order 4 ​ n 4n and prove that they achieve homotopy types which are not achieved by the presentations of Mannan–Popiel.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 57K20; Secondary 20C05, 57M05, 57Q12

1.
Introduction

Given a finitely presented group, can we classify its collection of finite presentations? To make this precise, we must first specify an equivalence relation on the class of finite presentations.
A finite presentation
\mathcal{P}
for a group
G
has an associated finite 2-complex
X_{\mathcal{P}}
with fundamental group
G
, and we say that two finite presentations are
homotopy equivalent
if their associated 2-complexes are.
The homotopy classification of finite
2
-complexes, and hence finite presentations, is inextricably linked to the D2 problem.
We say a CW-complex
X
is a
D2 complex
if it satisfies Wall’s D2 finiteness condition
[
Wal65
, p62]
:
H_{i}(\widetilde{X})=0
for
i>2
and
H^{3}(X;M)=0
for all finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]
-modules
M
.
The following can be found in
[
Wal79
, Problem D3]
.

D2 problem
.

Is every finite
D2
complex homotopy equivalent to a finite
2
-complex?

We say a finitely presented group
G
has the
D2 property
if every finite D2 complex
X
with
\pi_{1}(X)\cong G
is homotopy equivalent to a finite 2-complex.
Note that the D2 problem is closely related to the Eilenberg–Ganea conjecture and the Relation Gap problem (see
[
BT07
,
Har18
]
).

There is a one-to-one correspondence between the homotopy types of finite D2 complexes
X
with
\pi_{1}(X)\cong G
and the chain homotopy types of chain complexes of finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules
\mathcal{F}=(F_{2}\to F_{1}\to F_{0})
such that
H_{1}(\mathcal{F})=0
and
H_{0}(\mathcal{F})\cong\mathbb{Z}
(see
[
Nic21
, Theorem 2.1]
).
Thus, if
G
has the D2 property, then the homotopy classification of finite presentations for
G
reduces to the chain homotopy classification of chain complexes
\mathcal{F}
of the above form. This is often approachable using module-theoretic methods
[
Joh03
,
Joh12
,
Nic24a
]
.
The D2 problem is currently open, and the list of groups whose finite presentations have been classified up to homotopy currently coincides with the groups known to have the D2 property.

We say a pair of finite presentations for a group
G
are
exotic
if they have the same deficiency but are not homotopy equivalent.
Exotic presentations were first constructed for certain finite abelian groups
[
Met76
]
and the trefoil group
[
Dun76
]
. This led to much activity in the intervening years whereby finite abelian groups were shown to have the D2 property
[
Bro79a
]
, and the quaternion group
Q_{32}
was independently proposed as a counterexample by Cohen
[
Coh77
]
and Dyer
[
Dye78
]
.
Quaternion groups
Q_{4n}
of order
4n
were later studied and, for
2\leq n\leq 5
, it was shown that
Q_{4n}
admits no exotic presentations and satisfies the D2 property
[
Joh03
,
Joh04
]
.

Our main new contribution is the introduction of the following family of presentations

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}):=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},x^{n_{1}}yx^{m_{1}}y^{-1}\cdots x^{n_{k+1}}yx^{m_{k+1}}y^{-1}\rangle

for
n\geq 2
,
k\geq 0
,
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
n_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
and
m_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
. We will show:

(i)

Every presentation for
Q_{4n}
on the standard generating set of the form
\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
is homotopy equivalent to a presentation of the above form (
Theorem

3.1
).

(ii)

If
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k}
are all even, then
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};-n_{k},\dotsc,-n_{1})
presents
Q_{4n}
(
Theorem

3.9
).

The case
k=0
gives the standard presentation
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},xyxy^{-1}\rangle
for
Q_{4n}
.
Determining the
n_{i},m_{i}
for which
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
presents
Q_{4n}
appears to be difficult.
For example,
\mathcal{P}_{3}(2;2)
does not present
Q_{12}
(
3.12
) whilst
\mathcal{P}_{10}(3;5)
does present
Q_{40}
.

1.1.
Main results

Our main result on the D2 property for quaternion groups is as follows.
Recall that, if
G
is a finite group, then all exotic presentations have maximal deficiency
[
Bro79b
]
. Thus we restrict to presentations for
Q_{4n}
of deficiency zero, i.e. balanced presentations.

Theorem A
.

If
6\leq n\leq 8
, then
Q_{4n}
has the
D2
property and every balanced presentation is homotopy equivalent to precisely one of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;-2)
. Furthermore:

(i)

Q_{36}
has the
D2
property if and only if there exists a balanced presentation
\mathcal{P}
for
Q_{36}
such that
\mathcal{P}\not\simeq\mathcal{P}_{9}^{\std},\mathcal{P}_{9}(2;-2)
.

(ii)

Q_{40}
has the
D2
property if and only if there exist homotopically distinct balanced presentations
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
for
Q_{40}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{i}\not\simeq\mathcal{P}_{10}^{\std},\mathcal{P}_{10}(2;-2),\mathcal{P}_{10}(3;5)
for
i=1,2
.

The case
n=7
was established previously in
[
MP21
,
Nic21
]
and the family of presentations introduced above generalises the family
\mathcal{E}_{n,r}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},x^{r}yx^{2}yx^{1-r}y^{-1}x^{-1}y^{-1}\rangle
introduced by Mannan–Popiel
[
MP21
]
which is
\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;1-r)
in our notation. They showed that
\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;-2)
presents
Q_{4n}
for all
n\geq 2
, and that
\mathcal{P}_{7}^{\std}
,
\mathcal{P}_{7}(2;-2)
are exotic presentations for
Q_{28}
.
The case
n=8
implies that the CW-complex of Cohen and Dyer is homotopy equivalent to
X_{\mathcal{P}_{8}(2;-2)}
.

We now give a more detailed exploration of these presentations for quaternion groups of larger order, and their significance for the D2 problem.

Firstly, in
[
Nic24a
, Section 1.2]
we pointed out that the Mannan–Popiel presentations are such that
r\equiv s\,\,\text{mod}\,n
implies
\mathcal{E}_{n,r}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{n,s}
and so can represent at most
n
homotopy types of balanced presentations for
Q_{4n}
.
Since the number of corresponding chain homotopy types grows super-exponentially in
n
(see
[
Nic24a
, Theorem B]
), in order for
Q_{4n}
to have the D2 property, a presentation must exist which is not homotopy equivalent to one of this form.
We will show:

Theorem B
.

\mathcal{P}_{10}(3;5)=\langle x,y\mid x^{10}y^{-2},x^{3}yx^{5}y^{-1}x^{-2}yx^{-4}y^{-1}\rangle
is not homotopy equivalent to any presentation for
Q_{40}
of the form
\mathcal{P}_{10}(2;r)
for
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We show that, if
n_{i}\equiv n_{i}^{\prime}\,\,\text{mod}\,n
and
m_{i}\equiv m_{i}^{\prime}\,\,\text{mod}\,n
, then
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,n_{k}^{\prime};m_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,m_{k}^{\prime})
are homotopy equivalent (
Lemma

3.5
).
Thus, for fixed
k
, there are at most
n^{2k}
homotopy types of presentations. This is sub-exponential in
n
and so, in order to use these presentations to show that
Q_{4n}
has the D2 property, there must exist presentations for
k
arbitrarily large which do not reduce down to the case
k-1
up to homotopy.
We will show:

Theorem C
.

\mathcal{P}_{12}(2,2;-2,-2)=\langle x,y\mid x^{12}y^{-2},(x^{2}yx^{-2}y^{-1})^{2}x^{-3}yx^{5}y^{-1}\rangle
is not homotopy equivalent to any presentation for
Q_{48}
of the form
\mathcal{P}_{12}(n_{1};m_{1})
for
n_{1},m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

This gives some evidence to support the possibility that the missing presentations for
Q_{36}
and
Q_{40}
in
A
are of the form
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
for some
k\geq 2
, and thus that these groups have the D2 property.
The following seems plausible (cf.
[
MP21
, Conjecture 6.1]
):

Conjecture 1.1
.

If
n\geq 2
, then
Q_{4n}
has the
D2
property and every balanced presentation is homotopy equivalent to a presentation of the form
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
for some
n_{i},m_{i},k
.

By
[
Nic24a
, Theorem A]
, if
n\geq 6
and
Q_{4n}
has the D2 property, then
Q_{4n}
has an exotic presentation. We conjecture that this holds unconditionally, and we will show:

Theorem D
.

If
n=mk
where
6\leq m\leq 12
and
k\geq 1
is odd, then
Q_{4n}
has an exotic presentation. More specifically,
\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;-2)\not\simeq\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}
.

See
Section

4
for more detailed calculations. For example, we show that
\mathcal{P}_{12}^{\std}
,
\mathcal{P}_{12}(2;-2)
,
\mathcal{P}_{12}(4;-4)
and
\mathcal{P}_{12}(2,2;-2,-2)
are homotopically distinct (
4.22
).

1.2.
Methods and computational results

We will now give an overview of the ideas behind the proofs of Theorems
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
.

General strategy and the key new idea.

To determine whether
Q_{4n}
has the D2 property, we need a method for classifying chain complexes
\mathcal{F}
of the required form up to chain homotopy equivalence.
In previous work, we showed that there is a one-to-one correspondence
\Psi
between such chain complexes and finitely generated stably free
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-modules, considered up to isomorphism modulo a certain action of
\Aut(Q_{4n})

[
Nic24c
, Theorem B]
(see also
[
Joh03
,
Nic21
]
).

For each presentation
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
, we construct an ideal
I_{\mathcal{P}}\leq\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
which is stably free and represents
\Psi(C_{*}(X_{\mathcal{P}}))
(
Theorem

3.15
).
This reduces the question of whether two presentations
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are homotopy equivalent to the question of whether the corresponding ideals
I_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
I_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
are isomorphic modulo the action of
\Aut(Q_{4n})
. This is in contrast to the approach of Mannan–Popiel
[
MP21
]
whereby the
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-modules
\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}_{1}})
and
\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}_{2}})
are compared, and which is more difficult to determine since these modules are not projective.

Next, we need methods for determining whether two stably free
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-modules are isomorphic. Since these modules are projective, we can use the theory of Milnor squares to tackle this question using decompositions for the ring
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
in terms of certain pullback squares. In the case
Q_{4p}
for
p
prime (i.e.
Q_{28}
,
Q_{44}
), useful pullback square decompositions for the ring
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4p}
exist which allow us to classify projective
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4p}
-modules by computing double coset quotients of units in the various rings (
Section

4.3
). This applies to the cases
Q_{8p}
for
p
prime (i.e.
Q_{24}
,
Q_{40}
) and
Q_{4p^{2}}
for
p
prime (i.e.
Q_{36}
), albeit with more involved computations.
In contrast, the ring
\mathbb{Z}Q_{32}
does not admit such a nice pullback square decomposition and so we instead consider the extension of scalars of the ideals
I_{\mathcal{P}}
to an explicit maximal order
\Gamma\leq\mathbb{Q}Q_{32}
containing
\mathbb{Z}Q_{32}
. We then apply a freeness criterion established by Bley–Hofmann–Johnston (
4.10
).

Strategy for constructing presentations.

Lengthy computations are required in order to prove Theorems
B
and
C
. For example, in
C
, we must show that
\mathcal{P}_{12}(2,2;-2,-2)
is not homotopy equivalent to one of
13
presentations of the form
\mathcal{P}_{12}(n_{1};m_{1})
(
Lemma

4.23
).
Whilst these computations have been completed (
Section

4
), identifying presentations which actually have these properties proved a significant challenge. For example, before considering
\mathcal{P}_{12}(2,2;-2,-2)
, it makes sense to consider
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},n_{2};m_{1},m_{2})
for
n\in\{9,10,11\}
and various
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

In order to help with finding presentations with these properties, we used OSCAR
[
DEF+25
]
to decide whether two given stably free
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-modules are isomorphic with a high probability.
Algorithms for determining whether stably free
\mathbb{Z}Q_{4n}
-modules are isomorphic exist due to work of Bley–Johnston (see
[
BJ08
,
BJ11
,
HJ20
]
), but for large group orders (
4n>24
) these algorithms are too slow.
Instead we use a heuristic variant of these algorithms, which is much faster and predicts whether the modules are isomorphic, though has the drawback that the output of the algorithm is not a complete proof and so the results need to be recovered by hand.
Note that for
Q_{24}
, the Bley–Johnston algorithm can be used to verify our results.

Further results of heuristic computations.

The heuristic computations above can also be used to analyse
A
(i) and (ii). These computations suggest the following:

Conjecture 1.2
.

If
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{9}(n_{1},n_{2};m_{1},m_{2})
presents
Q_{36}
, then
\mathcal{P}\simeq\mathcal{P}_{9}^{\std}
or
\mathcal{P}_{9}(2;-2)
.

In the case of
Q_{40}
, the full computation for all presentations of the form
\mathcal{P}_{10}(n_{1},n_{2};m_{1},m_{2})
was too lengthy to complete but randomly selected presentations were all heuristically homotopy equivalent to the three presentations listed in
A
(ii).

This suggests that either the missing presentation for
Q_{36}
does not exist (implying that
Q_{36}
does not have the D2 property) or a presentation exists which is either of the form above for
k\geq 3
or which is not homotopy equivalent to any presentation of the form
\langle x,y\mid x^{9}y^{-2},R\rangle
.

1.3.
Further equivalence relations

Besides homotopy equivalence, there are several other natural equivalence relations which can be placed on the class of finite presentations.
We say that two finite presentations are
simple homotopy equivalent
(
\simeq_{s}
) if their associated
2
-complexes are simple homotopy equivalent in the sense of Whitehead (see
[
Coh73
]
). Two finite presentations are
generalised Andrews–Curtis equivalent
(
\simeq_{\AC}
), also known as
Q^{**}
-equivalent
[
HAM93
]
, if they are related by a sequence of operations of types (i)-(vi) which alter the generators and relators (see
[
HAM93
,
Bar25
]
).
These equivalence relations are related by
\simeq_{\AC}

\Rightarrow

\simeq_{s}

\Rightarrow

\simeq
.

For finite presentations, it is currently open whether
\simeq_{s}

\Rightarrow

\simeq_{\AC}
in general, and this is known as the generalised Andrews–Curtis conjecture.
In the case of the trivial group, this coincides with the usual Andrews–Curtis conjecture which asks whether all balanced presentations for the trivial group are Andrews–Curtis equivalent to the trivial presentation
\langle\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,\rangle

[
HAM93
]
.
Metzler
[
Met90
]
and Lustig
[
Lus91
]
independently constructed finite presentations which are homotopy but not simple homotopy equivalent. Their examples both presented infinite groups.

The family of presentations
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
present an interesting test case for simple homotopy equivalence and generalised Andrews–Curtis equivalence.
For each
n\geq 2
, there are infinitely many presentations for
Q_{4n}
of this form for
|n_{i}|,|m_{i}|<n
and
k\geq 0
(
Theorem

3.9
). On the other hand, since
Q_{4n}
is finite, there are only finitely many balanced presentations up to homotopy equivalence (this follows from the Jordan–Zassenhaus theorem
[
Swa70
]
). In fact, over
Q_{4n}
for
2\leq n\leq 5
, all these presentations are homotopy equivalent.

The difference between homotopy and simple homotopy is measured by an invariant lying in the Whitehead group
\Wh(Q_{4n})
. By
[
Oli88
]
, we have that
\Wh(Q_{8})=\Wh(Q_{12})=0
. Thus, over
Q_{8}
and
Q_{12}
, all these presentations are simple homotopy equivalent.
We ask:

Question 1.3
.

Do there exist presentations for
Q_{4n}
of the form
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
which are homotopy equivalent but not simple homotopy equivalent?

By
[
Oli88
]
,
\Wh(Q_{4n})
is infinite for
n\geq 4
so the possibility remains that there are infinitely many simple homotopy types of presentations in these cases. If so then, since there are only finitely many homotopy types, this would give the first examples of an infinite family of finite presentations which are all homotopy equivalent but pairwise not simple homotopy equivalent.
This would also give the first examples of finite groups with presentations which are homotopy equivalent but not simple homotopy equivalent.

If
1.3
had a negative answer and these presentations were also not counterexamples to the generalised Andrews–Curtis conjecture then, in light of the discussion above, there must exist
Q^{**}
-equivalences which reduce the value of
k
under certain conditions. However, we could not find any operations which alter the value of
k
except in the trivial case where one of
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k+1}
is equal to zero. We therefore ask:

Question 1.4
.

Do there exist presentations for
Q_{4n}
of the form
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
which are simple homotopy equivalent but not generalised Andrews–Curtis equivalent?

Organisation of the paper

The article will be structured as follows. In
Section

2
, we give the necessary background on modules, group presentations and the D2 problem. In
Section

3
, we introduce the presentations of the form
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
and establish their properties. In
Section

4
, we use these results to establish Theorems
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
.

2.
Preliminaries

We now develop the necessary preliminaries for the proof of the main results. In
Section

2.1
, we discuss
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules and pullback squares. In
Section

2.2
, we give algebraic methods for tackling the D2 problem in the case of quaternion groups. Finally, in
Section

2.3
, we show how to determine the chain complex
C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})
using Fox calculus.
Throughout this section, all
R
-modules will be taken to be left
R
-modules unless otherwise specified.

2.1.
Modules over integral group rings

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra. An
order in
A
is a subring
\Lambda\leq A
which is finitely generated as an abelian group and which has
\mathbb{Q}\cdot\Lambda=A
. For example, if
G
is a finite group, then
\mathbb{Q}G
is a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra and
\mathbb{Z}G
is an order in
\mathbb{Q}G
.
Note that, without specifying
A
, an
order
is simply a ring whose underlying abelian group is free of finite rank.

For a prime
p
and an order
\Lambda
, define a ring
\Lambda_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\Lambda
where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
denotes the
p
-adic integers. We say a
\Lambda
-module
M
is
locally free
if it is finitely generated and
M_{p}=\Lambda_{p}\otimes M
is a free
\Lambda_{p}
-module for all primes
p
.
Every locally free
\Lambda
-module is projective
[
Swa80
, Lemma 2.1]
. The converse does not hold in general but does hold in the case of
\mathbb{Z}G
for
G
a finite group, hence a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}G
-module is projective if and only if it is locally free
[
Swa80
, p156]
.

Throughout this article we will make frequent use of the following general lemma. This is presumably well known but we could not locate a suitable reference in the literature.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\Lambda
,
R
be orders and let
f:\Lambda\to R
be ring homomorphism.
If
I\leq\Lambda
is a locally free ideal, then
f_{\#}(I)
is isomorphic to the ideal
R\cdot f(I)\leq R
generated by
f(I)
. Furthermore, if
f
is surjective, then
f_{\#}(I)
is isomorphic to the ideal
f(I)\leq R
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes\Lambda
.
Since
I
is locally free, we have that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes I\cong\Lambda_{p}^{m}
for some
m
. Since
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a flat extension, the inclusion
I\hookrightarrow\Lambda
induces an inclusion
\Lambda_{p}^{m}\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{p}
. By considering
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-ranks this implies that
m\leq 1
since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is commutative and so satisfies the strong rank condition. If
m=0
, then
I=\{0\}
and the statement holds trivially, so we will assume that
m=1
. Thus the
\mathbb{Z}
-submodule
I
of
\Lambda
is of full rank, which is equivalent to
\Lambda/I
being finite.

Consider the exact sequence of
\Lambda
-modules:

0\to I\to\Lambda\to\Lambda/I\to 0.

Now apply
f_{\#}(\,\cdot\,)=R\otimes_{\Lambda}(\,\cdot\,)
to obtain an
exact sequence of
R
-modules

(1)

\Tor^{\Lambda}_{1}(\Lambda/I,R)\to f_{\#}(I)\to\underbrace{f_{\#}(\Lambda)}_{\cong R}\to f_{\#}(\Lambda/I)\to 0

where, in
\Tor^{\Lambda}_{1}(\Lambda/I,R)
, we view
R
as a right
\Lambda
-module via
f
. Since
I
is locally free, and hence projective,
f_{\#}(I)
is a projective
R
-module. Since the underlying abelian group of
R
is torsion free, this implies that
f_{\#}(I)
is torsion free as an abelian group. On the other hand,
\Tor^{\Lambda}_{1}(\Lambda/I,R)
is annihilated by
|\Lambda/I|<\infty
and so is torsion
[
Wei94
, Corollary 3.2.12]
. Hence the map
f_{\#}(I)\to R
in
Eq.

1
is injective. This completes the proof since the image is the ideal of
R
generated by
f(I)
.

The final part follows from the fact that, if
f
is surjective, then
f(I)=f(\Lambda\cdot I)=R\cdot f(I)
.
∎

For
\theta\in\Aut(G)
and a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
M
, let
M_{\theta}
denote the
\mathbb{Z}G
-module with the same underlying abelian group as
M
but with action
g\cdot m=\theta(g)m
for
g\in G
and
m\in M
. We can view
\theta\in\Aut(G)
as a ring isomorphism
\theta:\mathbb{Z}G\to\mathbb{Z}G
sending
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}g_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\theta(g_{i})
for
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
g_{i}\in G
.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
G
be a finite group and let
\theta\in\Aut(G)
. If
I=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\leq\mathbb{Z}G
is an ideal, then
I_{\theta}
is isomorphic to the ideal
(\theta^{-1}(x_{1}),\dotsc,\theta^{-1}(x_{n}))\leq\mathbb{Z}G
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

2.1
and the fact that
I_{\theta}\cong(\theta^{-1})_{\#}(I)

[
Nic24c
, Lemma 8.3]
.
∎

Using
Lemma

2.1
, we now give a specialisation of Milnor’s theory of projective modules over pullback squares
[
Mil71
, Chapter 2]
to the case of ideals.
We will restrict to pullback squares of the following standard type (see
[
Nic24b
, Section 2.3]
).
Let
\Lambda
be an order in a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
and let
A\cong A_{1}\times A_{2}
be an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras. For
i=1,2
, let
\Lambda_{i}=\Lambda/I_{i}
denote the projection of
\Lambda
onto
A_{i}
. Then there is a pullback square of rings

\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})=\hbox to84.08pt{\vbox to51.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.04161pt\lower-24.65279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -16.33 -23.51 L 20.22 -23.51}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.81528pt}{-16.99306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.20056pt}{-13.2792pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

where
\bar{\Lambda}=\Lambda/(I_{1}+I_{2})
is a finite ring and all maps are the natural surjections. For a ring
R
, let
P(R)
denote the set of isomorphism classes of finitely generated projective
R
-modules.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})
be as above. Then there is a bijection

\varphi:\{[P]\in P(\Lambda):(i_{1})_{\#}(P)\cong\Lambda_{1},(i_{2})_{\#}(P)\cong\Lambda_{2}\}\to\Lambda_{1}^{\times}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}^{\times}/\Lambda_{2}^{\times}.

If
I\leq\Lambda
is a projective
\Lambda
-ideal such that
i_{1}(I)=(\lambda_{1})\leq\Lambda_{1}
,
i_{2}(I)=(\lambda_{2})\leq\Lambda_{2}
and
j_{1}(\lambda_{1})\in\overline{\Lambda}^{\times}
, then
\varphi(I)=[j_{1}(\lambda_{1})j_{2}(\lambda_{2})^{-1}]
.

Proof.

The existence of such a bijection follows from
[
Mil71
, Chapter 2]
, so we will just prove the statement about the projective ideal.
Since
i_{k}
is surjective for
k=1,2
,
Lemma

2.1
implies that
(i_{k})_{\#}(I)\cong i_{k}(I)=(\lambda_{k})
. Thus
(i_{k})_{\#}(I)\cong\Lambda_{k}
since it is singly generated and projective. Thus
I
is in the domain of
\varphi
.
Note that
(j_{2}(\lambda_{2}))=(j_{2}\circ i_{2})(I)=(j_{1}\circ i_{1})(I)=(j_{1}(\lambda_{1}))=\overline{\Lambda}
since
j_{1}(\lambda_{1})\in\overline{\Lambda}^{\times}
. Hence
j_{2}(\lambda_{2})\in\overline{\Lambda}^{\times}
and so we have that
j_{1}(\lambda_{1})j_{2}(\lambda_{2})^{-1}\in\overline{\Lambda}^{\times}
.

Since
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
are surjective,
\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})
is a Milnor square and so results in
[
Mil71
, Chapter 2]
imply there exists a bijection
\varphi
such that, if
u\in\bar{\Lambda}^{\times}
, then
\varphi^{-1}(u)=[M(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},u)]
where

M(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},u):=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}:j_{1}(x_{1})u=j_{2}(x_{2})\}\leq\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}.

We view
\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}
as a
\Lambda
-module under the action
\lambda\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})=(i_{1}(\lambda)x_{1},i_{2}(\lambda)x_{2})
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
, and
M(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},u)
is a
\Lambda
-submodule of
\Lambda_{1}\times\Lambda_{2}
.

It now suffices to prove that, if
u=j_{1}(\lambda_{1})j_{2}(\lambda_{2})^{-1}
, then there is an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-modules
I\cong M(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},u)
.
Note that there is a
\Lambda
-module homomorphism
g:I\to M(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},u)
such that
g(x)=(x_{1},x_{2})
where
i_{k}(x)=x_{k}\lambda_{k}
for each
k=1,2
. This is well-defined since
j_{1}\circ i_{1}=j_{2}\circ i_{2}
implies that
j_{1}(x_{1})j_{1}(\lambda_{1})=j_{2}(x_{2})j_{2}(\lambda_{2})
and so
j_{1}(x_{1})u=j_{2}(x_{2})
. This is bijective since
\mathcal{R}(\Lambda,A_{1},A_{2})
is a pullback square.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

We have the following variant of Lemma
2.3
, which can be proven similarly (see
[
Swa80
, §4]
).
Let
Q
be a projective
\Lambda
-module,
Q_{k}=i_{k}(Q)
,
k=1,2
and
\overline{Q}=(j_{1})_{\#}(Q_{1})\cong(j_{2})_{\#}(Q_{2})
. Then there is a bijection

\varphi:\{[P]\in P(\Lambda):(i_{1})_{\#}(P)\cong Q_{1},(i_{2})_{\#}(P)\cong Q_{2}\}\to\Aut(Q_{1})\backslash{\Aut(\bar{Q})}/{\Aut(Q_{2})}.

If
P
is a projective
\Lambda
-module,
\bar{P}=(j_{1}\circ i_{1})_{\#}(P)\cong(j_{2}\circ i_{2})_{\#}(P)
,
f_{k}\colon Q_{k}\to(i_{k})_{\#}(P)
isomorphisms with induced isomorphisms
\bar{f}_{k}\colon\bar{Q}\to\bar{P}
, then
\varphi([P])=[\bar{f}_{2}^{-1}\bar{f}_{1}]
.

We next introduce a special class of modules over
\mathbb{Z}G
for
G
a finite group.
A
Swan module
is a
\mathbb{Z}G
-ideal of the form
(N,r)\leq\mathbb{Z}G
where
N=\sum_{g\in G}g
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
is an integer coprime to
|G|
. This is projective and only depends up to isomorphism on
r\,\,\text{mod}\,|G|
, so we can view
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
.
Swan modules are also defined as
(I_{G},r)\leq\mathbb{Z}G
where
I_{G}
is the augmentation ideal. These definitions coincide since
(I_{G},r)\cong(N,r^{-1})
for all
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}

[
Nic24c
, Proposition 3.4]
.

If
I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
is an ideal then, since
(N,r)
is a two-sided ideal and hence a
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodule, we can form a (left)
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
(N,r)\otimes I
. This is isomorphic to the ideal
N\cdot I+r\cdot I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
and let
\theta\in\Aut(G)
. Then
(N,r)_{\theta}\cong(N,r)
. More generally, if
I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
is an ideal, then
((N,r)\otimes I)_{\theta}\cong(N,r)\otimes I_{\theta}
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

2.2
, we have
(N,r)_{\theta}\cong(\theta^{-1}(N),\theta^{-1}(r))=(N,r)
since
N,r
are invariant under the action of
\Aut(G)
. Let
I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
be an ideal. Since
\mathbb{Z}G
is Noetherian, we have
I=(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
for some
x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}G
, and so
N\cdot I+r\cdot I=(Nx_{1},\dotsc,Nx_{n},rx_{1},\dotsc,rx_{n})
. Hence:

\displaystyle((N,r)\otimes I)_{\theta}

\displaystyle\cong(\theta^{-1}(Nx_{1}),\dotsc,\theta^{-1}(Nx_{n}),\theta^{-1}(rx_{1}),\dotsc,\theta^{-1}(rx_{n}))

\displaystyle=(N\theta^{-1}(x_{1}),\dotsc,N\theta^{-1}(x_{n}),r\theta^{-1}(x_{1}),\dotsc,r\theta^{-1}(x_{n}))\cong(N,r)\otimes I_{\theta}.\qed

We now define the dual of a left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
M
. Firstly, let
M^:=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(M,\mathbb{Z}G)
denote the right
\mathbb{Z}G
-module with the standard action
\varphi\cdot\lambda:m\mapsto\varphi(m)\lambda
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}G
,
\varphi\in M^
and
m\in M
.
Secondly, given a right
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
N
, define
c(N)
to be the left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module with underlying abelian group
N
and action
\lambda\cdot m:=m\cdot_{N}\bar{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}G
,
m\in M
where
\cdot_{N}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}G
-action on
N
and
\overline{\cdot}:\mathbb{Z}G\to\mathbb{Z}G
is the involution
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}g_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}g_{i}^{-1}
.
For a left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
M
, we define the
dual
of
M
to be the left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
M^{*}:=c(M^)
(see
[
CR62
, p318]
).

Remark 2.6
.

Many authors use the notation
M^
to refer to
M^{*}
and vice versa. We chose the notation above since the left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module dual
M^{*}
is the primary object of interest in this article.

For a left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
N
, we can define a right
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
c(N)
similarly. If
N
is a
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodule, we define
c(N)
to be the
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodule with action
\lambda\cdot x\cdot\mu:=\bar{\mu}\cdot_{N}x\cdot_{N}\bar{\lambda}
for
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}G
and
x\in N
.
For a right
\mathbb{Z}G
-module (resp.
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodule)
M
, we can also define left
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules (resp.
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodules)
M^
and
M^{*}=c(M^)
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
M
be a
(\mathbb{Z}G,\mathbb{Z}G)
-bimodule and
N
a left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module. Then there is an isomorphism of left
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules
(M\otimes N)^{*}\cong M^{*}\otimes N^{*}
.

Proof.

We begin by noting that there is an isomorphism
f:c(N^\otimes M^)\to M^{*}\otimes N^{*}
given by sending
\varphi\otimes\psi\mapsto\psi\otimes\varphi
for
\varphi\in N^
and
\psi\in M^
. This is bijective and is a left
\mathbb{Z}G
-module homomorphism since, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}G
,
\varphi\in N^
and
\psi\in M^
we have

f(\lambda\cdot_{c(N^\otimes M^)}(\varphi\otimes\psi))=f(\varphi\otimes(\psi\bar{\lambda}))=(\psi\bar{\lambda})\otimes\varphi=(\lambda\cdot_{M^{*}}\psi)\otimes\varphi=\lambda\cdot_{M^{*}\otimes N^{*}}f(\varphi\otimes\psi).

Since
(M\otimes N)^{*}\cong c((M\otimes N)^)
, it now suffices to prove that
N^\otimes M^\cong(M\otimes N)^
as right
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules.
By
[
CR81
, (2.29)]
(particularly the formulation given in 2.32), the map

N^\otimes M^\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(N,M^),\varphi\otimes\psi\mapsto(x\mapsto\varphi(x)\cdot\psi)

is an isomorphism of abelian groups, for which it is straightforward to verify that it is an isomorphism of right
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules.
By
[
CR81
, (2.19)]
the map

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(N,M^)\to(M\otimes N)^,\varphi\mapsto((x\otimes y)\mapsto(\varphi(y))(x)

is an isomorphism of abelian groups, and we can similarly verify this is an isomorphism of right
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules.
Thus
N^\otimes M^\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(N,M^)\cong(M\otimes N)^
, as required.
∎

2.2.
Finite 2-complexes over groups with free period 4

For a finitely presented group
G
, let
\HT(G)
denote the set of homotopy types of finite
2
-complexes
X
with
\pi_{1}(X)\cong G
. This can be viewed as a graph with edges between each
X
and
X\vee S^{2}
. It is well-known that
\HT(G)
is a tree and has a grading coming from
\chi(X)
which has a minimum value
\chi_{\min}(G)
(see, for example,
[
Nic24c
, p2245]
).

In the case where
G
is finite, it was shown by W. H. Browning
[
Bro79b
]
(see
[
HK93
]
for a published proof) that
X\simeq Y
provided
\chi(X)=\chi(Y)>\chi_{\min}(G)
and that there are finitely many
X
up to homotopy with
\chi(X)=\chi_{\min}(X)
. In particular,
\HT(G)
has the following form:

\vdots

Figure 1.
Tree structure for
\HT(G)
when
G
is a finite group

Suppose
G
is a finite group with 4-periodic cohomology, i.e. the Tate cohomology groups satisfy
\widehat{H}^{i}(G;\mathbb{Z})\cong\widehat{H}^{i+4}(G;\mathbb{Z})
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is equivalent to the existence of a 4-periodic projective resolution of
\mathbb{Z}

[
CE56
, XII]
. We say
G
has
free period
4
if there exists a 4-periodic free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
, or equivalently an exact sequence of finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules

(2)

F=(0\to\mathbb{Z}\to F_{3}\to F_{2}\to F_{1}\to F_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0)

where each
F_{i}
is free. For example,
Q_{4n}
has free period 4 for all
n\geq 2

[
CE56
, p253]
.

Let
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
denote the set of isomorphism classes of non-zero finitely generated stably free
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules.
It follows from
[
Nic24c
, Theorem B]
that, if
G
has free period
4
, then there is an injective map
\Psi
from
\HT(G)
to the set of orbits of
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
by a certain non-standard action of
\Aut(G)
on
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
, and
\Psi
is bijective if and only if
G
has the D2 property.

Let
\HT_{\min}(G)=\{X\in\HT(G):\chi(X)=\chi_{\min}(X)\}
and let
\SF_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
denote the set of isomorphism classes of rank one stably free
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules. Since
\HT(G)
is as in
Fig.

1
,
\Psi
is bijective if and only if the restricted map from
\HT_{\min}(G)
to the orbits of
\SF_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is bijective.

One drawback with this result is that both the action of
\Aut(G)
on
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
and the map
\Psi
itself are difficult to determine in specific examples.
We circumvent these difficulties by quotienting
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
by an equivalence relation which is a priori weaker than the action of
\Aut(G)
. We use that every non-zero finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module is of the form
I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
where
I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
is an ideal and
n\geq 0

[
Swa60
, Theorem A]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For
P
,
Q\in\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
, say
P\sim Q
if there exist ideals
I,J\leq\mathbb{Z}G
and
n\geq 0
such that
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
,
Q\cong J\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
and
I\cong(N,r)\otimes J_{\theta}
for some
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
,
\theta\in\Aut(G)
.
Then:

(i)

\sim
is an equivalence relation on
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
.

(ii)

If
I\cong(N,r)\otimes J_{\theta}
as above, then
(N,r)
is stably free. In particular, if stably free Swan modules are free, then
\sim
coincides with isomorphism under the action of
\Aut(G)
.

(iii)

If
P\sim Q
, then
P^{*}\sim Q^{*}
. More specifically, if
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
,
Q\cong J\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
and
I\cong(N,r)\otimes J_{\theta}
, then
I^{*}\cong(N,r^{-1})\otimes(J^{*})_{\theta}
.

Proof.

(i) Reflexivity is clear. Note that, if
r,s\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
, then
(N,r)\otimes(N,s)\cong(N,rs)
. For symmetry, if
P\sim Q
, then
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
,
Q\cong J\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
and
I\cong(N,r)\otimes J_{\theta}
which implies that

(N,r^{-1})\otimes I_{\theta^{-1}}\cong(N,r^{-1})\otimes((N,r)\otimes J_{\theta})_{\theta^{-1}}\cong(N,r^{-1})\otimes(N,r)\otimes(J_{\theta})_{\theta^{-1}}\cong(N,r^{-1}r)\otimes J\cong J

where we have used
Lemma

2.5
for the second isomorphism, and the fact that
r^{-1}r\equiv 1\,\,\text{mod}\,|G|
implies that
(N,r^{-1}r)\cong(N,1)=\mathbb{Z}G
for the fourth.
Transitivity follows similarly.

(ii) Since
P
is stably free,
P_{\theta}
is stably free. By
[
Nic24c
, Lemma 4.15]
, we have
[Q]=[(N,r)]+[P_{\theta}]\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
. Since
[Q]=[P_{\theta}]=0
, we have that
[(N,r)]=0
, i.e.
(N,r)
is stably free.

(iii) First note that, if
I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
is an ideal, then
((N,r)\otimes I)^{*}\cong(N,r)^{*}\otimes I^{*}
by
Lemma

2.7
.
Next note that
(N,r)^{*}\cong(I_{G},r)\cong(N,r^{-1})
by combining
[
Swa83
, Lemma 17.1]
with
[
Nic24c
, Proposition 3.4]
, and we have
(I_{\theta})^{*}\cong(I^{*})_{\theta}
(see, for example, the proof of
[
Nic24c
, Lemma 6.3]
). Hence, if
I\cong(N,r)\otimes J_{\theta}
, then
I^{*}\cong(N,r)^{*}\otimes(J_{\theta})^{*}\cong(N,r^{-1})\otimes(J^{*})_{\theta}
, as required.
∎

The following gives weaker yet easily computable analogue of
\Psi
(see (i)) and also computes
\Psi
in a special case (see (ii)). Here
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/{\Aut(G)}
denotes the set of orbits of the standard action of
\Aut(G)
on
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
, where
\theta\in\Aut(G)
sends
P\mapsto P_{\theta}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
G
have free period
4
. Then:

(i)

There is a map
\widehat{\Psi}:\HT(G)\to\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/{\sim}
where
\widehat{\Psi}(X)=[P]
for any projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
P
such that there is an exact sequence of the form
0\to\mathbb{Z}\to P\to\pi_{2}(X)\to 0
.

(ii)

If stably free Swan modules over
\mathbb{Z}G
are free, then there is an injective map
\Psi:\HT(G)\to\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/{\Aut(G)}
where
\Psi(X)=[P]
for any projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
P
such that there is an exact sequence of the form
0\to\mathbb{Z}\to P\to\pi_{2}(X)\to 0
. Furthermore,
\Psi
is bijective if and only if
G
has the
D2
property.

Proof.

We begin by recalling the bijection given by
[
Nic24c
, Theorem B]
in more detail. First fix a 4-periodic free resolution
F
as in
Eq.

2
.
For
P
,
Q\in\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
, say
P\equiv Q
if there exist ideals
I,J\leq\mathbb{Z}G
and
n\geq 0
such that
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
,
Q\cong J\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
and
I\cong(N,\psi_{4}(\theta))\otimes J_{\theta}
for some
\theta\in\Aut(G)
, where
\psi_{4}:\Aut(G)\to(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
is a group homomorphism which depends only on
G
(see
[
Nic24c
, Section 7]
for a definition).
This is an equivalence relation and, by
[
Nic24c
, Theorem 5.3 (i)]
, there is an injective map

\Psi_{F}:\HT(G)\to\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/\equiv\,,\quad X\mapsto[P_{X,F}]

where
P_{X,F}
is the unique projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module such that there is a chain homotopy equivalence

(0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\alpha}P_{X,F}\xrightarrow{\beta}\pi_{2}(X)\to 0)\circ C_{*}(\widetilde{X})\simeq F

for some
\alpha
,
\beta
, and where
\circ
denotes Yoneda product. Note that
P_{X,F}
is necessarily stably free.

(i) For
P
,
Q\in\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
, it is clear that
P\equiv Q
implies
P\sim Q
. Define
\widehat{\Psi}
to be the composition of
\Psi_{F}
and the quotient map
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/\equiv\,\twoheadrightarrow\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/{\sim}
.
Let
P
be any projective
\mathbb{Z}G
module such that there is an exact sequence of the form
0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\alpha}P\xrightarrow{\beta}\pi_{2}(X)\to 0
for some maps
\alpha
,
\beta
. We claim that
\widehat{\Psi}(X)=[P]
which, in particular, implies that
\widehat{\Psi}
does not depend on the choice of
F
.
It suffices to prove that
P\sim P_{X,F}
.

Let
F^{\prime}=(0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\alpha}P\xrightarrow{\beta}\pi_{2}(X)\to 0)\circ C_{*}(\widetilde{X})
.
Let
\Proj_{\mathbb{Z}G}^{4}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
denote the set of
4
-periodic projective resolutions of
\mathbb{Z}
up to congruence (see
[
Nic24c
, p2249]
), which is a weaker notion than chain homotopy equivalence.
For any
E\in\Proj_{\mathbb{Z}G}^{4}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
, there is a bijection

(m_{\cdot})^{*}:(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}\to\Proj_{\mathbb{Z}G}^{4}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}),\quad r\mapsto(m_{r})^{*}(E)

where
m_{r}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
denotes multiplication by
r
, and
(\,\cdot\,)^{*}
denotes the pullback of exact sequences
[
Nic24c
, Proposition 3.5]
.
Let
E
be the dual extension

E=(F^{\prime})^{*}=C_{*}(\widetilde{X})^{*}\circ(0\to\pi_{2}(X)^{*}\xrightarrow{\beta^{*}}P^{*}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{*}}\mathbb{Z}\to 0).

Then there exists
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
such that
(m_{r})^{*}(E)\simeq F^{*}
. By
[
Nic24c
, Lemma 4.12]
, we have

\displaystyle F^{*}\simeq(m_{r})^{*}(E)

\displaystyle\simeq C_{*}(\widetilde{X})^{*}\circ(m_{r})^{*}(0\to\pi_{2}(X)^{*}\xrightarrow{\beta^{*}}P^{*}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{*}}\mathbb{Z}\to 0)

\displaystyle\simeq C_{*}(\widetilde{X})^{*}\circ(0\to\pi_{2}(X)^{*}\xrightarrow{\gamma}Q\xrightarrow{\delta}\mathbb{Z}\to 0)

for some
\gamma
,
\delta
and where, if
P^{*}\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
for an ideal
I\leq\mathbb{Z}G
, then
Q\cong((I_{G},r)\otimes I)\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
. By
[
Nic24c
, Proposition 3.4]
,
(I_{G},r)\cong(N,r^{-1})
and so
Q^{*}\cong((N,r^{-1})\otimes I)\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
.
Dualising the sequence above now gives that

F\simeq(0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\delta^{*}}Q^{*}\xrightarrow{\gamma^{*}}\pi_{2}(X)\to 0)\circ C_{*}(\widetilde{X})

and so
P_{X,F}=Q^{*}
. By
Lemma

2.8
(iii), we have
Q^{*}\cong((N,r)\otimes I^{*})\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
. Since
P\cong I^{*}\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
and
I^{*}
is isomorphic to a
\mathbb{Z}G
-ideal, this implies that
P_{X,F}=Q^{*}\sim P
, as required.

(ii) If stably free Swan modules are free,
Lemma

2.8
(ii) implies that the three equivalence relations we have considered on
\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)
all coincide:
\sim
,
\equiv
and equivalence under the standard action of
\Aut(G)
. Hence
\Psi:=\Psi_{F}=\widehat{\Psi}:\HT(G)\to\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/{\Aut(G)}
is injective and of the required form.
By
[
Nic24c
, Theorem B]
,
\Psi
is bijective if and only if
G
has the D2 property.
∎

If
\mathcal{P}
is a presentation for a finite group
G
, then we can write
\Psi(\mathcal{P})
to denote
\Psi(X_{\mathcal{P}})
, where
X_{\mathcal{P}}
is the presentation 2-complex associated to
\mathcal{P}
.

Example 2.10
.

Let
n\geq 2
and let
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}
denote the standard presentation for
G=Q_{4n}
. Then
\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}})\cong\mathbb{Z}G/N
by comparing
C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}})
with the resolution in
[
CE56
, p253]
. There is an exact sequence
0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\cdot N}\mathbb{Z}G\to\mathbb{Z}G/N\to 0
and so
\widehat{\Psi}(\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std})=[\mathbb{Z}G]
.

2.3.
Group presentations and Fox calculus

Suppose
\mathcal{P}=\langle x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\mid r_{1},\dotsc,r_{m}\rangle
is a finite presentation for a group
G
. We can transform
\mathcal{P}
into another finite presentation for
G
using the following moves:

(i)

Replacing a relator
r_{i}
by
r_{i}r_{j}
for some
i\neq j
.

(ii)

Replacing a relator
r_{i}
by
r_{i}^{-1}
.

(iii)

Replacing a relator
r_{i}
by
wr_{i}w^{-1}
for some
w\in F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
.

(iv)

Replacing each relator
r_{i}
by
\phi(r_{i})
for some
\phi\in\Aut(F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}))
.

(v)

Add a generator
x_{n+1}
and a relator
r_{m+1}
which coincides with
x_{n+1}
.

(vi)

The inverse of (v), when possible.

The moves (i)-(iii) are
Q
-transformations, the moves (i)-(iv) are
Q^{*}
-transformations and the moves (i)-(vii) are
Q^{**}
-transformations.
Let
R=Q
,
Q^{*}
or
Q^{**}
. We say two finite presentations
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{Q}
are
R
-equivalent
, written
\mathcal{P}\simeq_{R}\mathcal{Q}
, if they are related via a sequence of
R
-transformations.

If
\mathcal{P}
has
n
generators and
m
relations, then define the
deficiency

\Def(\mathcal{P})=n-m
. This is a homotopy invariant since
\chi(X_{\mathcal{P}})=1-\Def(\mathcal{P})
. Recall that we defined what it means for two finite presentations to be homotopy (resp. simple homotopy) equivalent in the introduction.
It is straightforward to see that the
Q^{**}
-transformations induce simple homotopy equivalences and so we have the following chain of implications:

\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q^{*}}\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q^{**}}\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}\simeq_{s}\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}\simeq\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow\Def(\mathcal{P})=\Def(\mathcal{Q}).

We will now define Fox differentiation of elements in a free group, and use this to describe the boundary maps in the chain complex
C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})
, where
\mathcal{P}
is a finite presentation for a group
G
.
We will follow
[
Fox53
]
and
[
LS77
, II.3]
(see also
[
Har18
, Section 1.2]
).

Let
X=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\}
be a finite set and let
F(X)
denote the free group on
X
. Then the
Fox derivative
with respect to
x_{i}
is the homomorphism of abelian groups

\partial_{x_{i}}:\mathbb{Z}F(X)\to\mathbb{Z}F(X)

which is defined by the requirements that
\partial_{x_{i}}(x_{j})=\delta_{ij}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta function, and the product rule
\partial_{x_{i}}(uv)=\partial_{x_{i}}(u)+u\partial_{x_{i}}(v)
for any
u,v\in F(X)
.

More generally, if
G
is a group with generating set
X=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\}
, we have an implicit surjection
\phi:F(X)\twoheadrightarrow G
. From this, we obtain a map
\partial_{x_{i}}(G):\mathbb{Z}F(X)\to\mathbb{Z}G
by post-composing with
\phi
. The special case where
G=F(X)
coincides with the original definition above. We will write
\partial_{x_{i}}=\partial_{x_{i}}(G)
when the choice of
G
is clear from the context.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\langle x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}\mid r_{1},\dotsc,r_{m}\rangle
be a presentation for
G
, and let
C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})
denote the corresponding chain complex of
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules. Then
C_{0}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\cong\mathbb{Z}G
,
C_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\cong\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
with basis denoted by
e(x_{i})
, and
C_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\cong\mathbb{Z}G^{m}
with basis denoted by
e(r_{i})
. Then, with respect to these identifications, we have

C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})=(C_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\partial_{2}}C_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}C_{0}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}}))

where
\partial_{2}(e(r_{i}))=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\partial_{x_{j}}(r_{i})e(x_{j})
and
\partial_{1}(e(x_{i}))=x_{i}-1
.

We have that
\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}})\cong\pi_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\cong H_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\cong\Ker(\partial_{2})
. For the sake of this article, we will therefore just define
\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}})=\Ker(\partial_{2})
. Since
H_{0}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, the chain complex
C_{*}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})
extends to an exact sequence

0\to\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}})\hookrightarrow C_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\partial_{2}}C_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}C_{0}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathbb{Z}\to 0

where
\varepsilon:\mathbb{Z}G\to\mathbb{Z}
denotes the augmentation map.

We conclude this section by recalling two useful properties of Fox derivatives. The first is a straightforward consequence of the product rule
[
Fox53
, (2.5)]
and the second is
[
Fox53
, (2.6)]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
X=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\}
and let
w=w_{0}x_{i}^{n_{1}}w_{1}x_{i}^{n_{2}}\dotsm w_{k-1}x_{i}^{n_{k}}w_{k}\in F(X)
where
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and each
w_{i}\in F(X)
does not involve the generator
x_{i}
. Then

\partial_{x_{i}}w=\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{0}x_{i}^{n_{1}}w_{1}x_{i}^{n_{2}}\dotsm w_{j-1}\partial_{x_{i}}(x_{i}^{n_{j}})\in\mathbb{Z}F(X).

Proposition 2.13

(Chain rule for Fox derivatives)
.

Let
X=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\}
, let
Y=\{y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m}\}
and let
f:F(Y)\to F(X)
be a homomorphism. If
w\in F(Y)
, then

\partial_{x_{i}}f(w)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(\partial_{y_{j}}w)\partial_{x_{i}}(f(y_{j}))\in\mathbb{Z}F(X).

3.
Regular presentations for quaternion groups

In this section, we will study presentations for quaternion groups of the form
\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
on the standard generating set. In
Section

3.1
, we show how
Q
-transformations can be used to put such presentations in the form
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
(
Theorem

3.1
), and we establish basic properties of these presentations in
Section

3.2
.
In
Section

3.3
, we give a formula for the stably free
\mathbb{Z}G
-module such a presentation corresponds to via the map
\Psi
defined in
Section

2.2
(
Theorem

3.15
) and,
in
Section

3.4
, we make this formula more precise for particular examples.

3.1.
Standard form for regular presentations

Let
F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
denote the free group of rank
n
on generators
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}
.
Suppose
R\in F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
and
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n}\in F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
. Then we define
R(w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n})\in F(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
by replacing each appearance of
x_{i}
in
R
by
w_{n}
.

Fix
n\geq 2
and fix an identification
Q_{4n}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},xyxy^{-1}\rangle
.
Define a
regular
presentation to be a presentation of the form
\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
for some
R\in F(x,y)
which presents
Q_{4n}
on the standard generating set, i.e.
\id:\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\to Q_{4n}
,
x\mapsto x,y\mapsto y
is a group isomorphism.

For integers
k\geq 0
and
n_{1},m_{1},\dots,n_{k},m_{k}\neq 0
, define

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}):=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle

where
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k+1}}b^{m_{k+1}}
for
n_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
and
m_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
.
We say that
k
is the
height
of the presentation. If
k=0
then
R=ab
and so we obtain the standard presentation
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},yxy^{-1}x\rangle
for
Q_{4n}
.
The presentations
\mathcal{E}_{n,r}
of Mannan–Popiel have height one and coincide with
\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;1-r)
.
We now aim to prove the following.

Theorem 3.1
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a regular presentation for
Q_{4n}
, then there exist integers
k\geq 0
and
n_{1},m_{1},\dots,n_{k},m_{k}\neq 0
such that

\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
.

We will begin by establishing basic operations on the second relator which preserve the
Q
-type.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
be a presentation for
Q_{4n}
for some
R\in F(x,y)
. Suppose
R^{\prime}\in F(x,y)
has one of the following forms:

(i)

R^{\prime}
is a cyclic permutation of
R
.

(ii)

R^{\prime}
is formed from
R
by replacing
y^{\pm 2}\subseteq R
with
x^{\pm n}\subseteq R
.

(iii)

R^{\prime}
is formed from
R
by moving a subword
y^{\pm 2}\subseteq R
(or
x^{\pm n}\subseteq R
) to a different place in
R
.

Then
\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R^{\prime}\rangle
.

Proof.

(i) This is a
Q
-move of type (iii) since cyclically permuting
R
corresponds to conjugation.

(ii) First cyclically permute
R
so that
y^{\pm 2}
is the leading term. Then alter the first relator to
x^{\pm n}y^{\mp 2}
(using moves of type (ii) and (iii) if necessary). Then use a move of type (i) to replace
R
with
x^{\pm n}y^{\mp 2}R
, which simplifies to
R
with
y^{\pm 2}
replaced with
x^{\pm n}
. Now cyclically permute
R
again to move it back in its place. Clearly the same operation works in reverse, i.e. we can replace a
x^{\pm n}
with a
y^{\pm 2}
.

(iii)
By part (ii), we can move
y^{\pm 2}\subseteq R
anywhere in
R
by switching it between
y^{\pm 2}
and
x^{\pm n}
whenever it needs to move past a power of
y
or
x
respectively.
∎

This allows us to prove the following, which resolves part of
Theorem

3.1
.

Proposition 3.3
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a regular presentation for
Q_{4n}
, then there exists
R\in F(a,b)
such that
\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle
where
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k}}b^{m_{k}}
for some
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

We will write
R\simeq R^{\prime}
for
R,R^{\prime}\in F(x,y)
if
\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\simeq_{Q}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R^{\prime}\rangle
.
By abuse of notation, we will continue referring to the second relator as
R
after the presentation has been altered by a
Q
-transformation.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
be a regular presentation for
Q_{4n}
for some
R\in F(x,y)
. Let
R=x^{n_{1}}y^{m_{1}}\cdots x^{n_{k}}y^{m_{k}}
for some
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and some
k\geq 1
. By
Lemma

3.2
(ii), we can replace any
y^{2}\subseteq R
with
x^{n}\subseteq R
. If
m_{i}=2m
is even, then replace
y^{m_{i}}
with
x^{nm_{i}}
. If
m_{i}=2m+1
is odd, then replace
y^{m_{i}}
with
x^{nm_{i}}y
. After relabelling the
n_{i}
and
k
, this gives that
R\simeq x^{n_{1}}y\cdots x^{n_{k}}y
.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is regular, we have that
\id:\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\to Q_{4n}
is a group isomorphism. In particular,
x^{n_{1}}y\cdots x^{n_{k}}y=1
holds in
Q_{4n}
. It follows that
x^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i+1}n_{i}}y^{k}=1
and so
k
must be even. We can therefore write
R
in the form

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=(x^{n_{1}}yx^{m_{1}}y)\cdots(x^{n_{k}}yx^{m_{k}}y)\simeq x^{nk}(x^{n_{0}}yx^{m_{1}}y^{-1})\cdots(x^{n_{k}}yx^{m_{k}}y^{-1})

\displaystyle=x^{n_{1}+nk}(yxy^{-1})^{m_{1}}x^{n_{2}}(yxy^{-1})^{m_{2}}\cdots x^{n_{k}}(yxy^{-1})^{m_{k}}.

By relabelling the
n_{i}
and
m_{i}
, we have
R=S(x,yxy^{-1})
for
S=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k}}b^{m_{k}}\in F(a,b)
. ∎

We now give operations on the words
R\in F(a,b)
which preserve the
Q
-type of presentations of the form given in
3.3
. They each follow from
Lemma

3.2
.
Moves (i), (ii) and (iv) will be used in the proof but (iii) and (v) will be stated here for later use (see
Lemma

3.5
).

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle
be a presentation for
Q_{4n}
for
R\in F(a,b)
and
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k}}b^{m_{k}}
for some
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k\geq 1
.
Suppose
R^{\prime}\in F(a,b)
is the result of one of the following operations of
R
:

(i)

For some
1\leq i,j\leq k
,
i\neq j
, replace
n_{i}
with
n_{i}\pm n
and
n_{j}
with
n_{j}\mp n
.

(ii)

For some
1\leq i,j\leq k
, replace
n_{i}
with
n_{i}\pm n
and
m_{j}
with
m_{j}\mp n
.

(iii)

Cyclically permuting the pairs
(n_{i},m_{i})
, i.e.
R^{\prime}=a^{n_{2}}b^{m_{2}}\cdots a^{n_{k}}b^{m_{k}}a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}
.

(iv)

Inversion followed by a cyclic permutation, i.e.
R^{\prime}=a^{-n_{1}}b^{-m_{k}}a^{-n_{k}}b^{-m_{k-1}}\cdots a^{-n_{2}}b^{-m_{1}}
.

(v)

Regrouping of terms, i.e.
R^{\prime}=a^{m_{1}}b^{n_{2}}a^{m_{2}}b^{n_{3}}\cdots a^{m_{k-1}}b^{n_{k}}a^{m_{k}}b^{n_{1}}
.

Then
\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R^{\prime}(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

We will write
R\simeq R^{\prime}
for
R,R^{\prime}\in F(a,b)
if

\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle\simeq_{Q}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R^{\prime}(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle.

For convenience, we will continue referring to the relator as
R
after the presentation has been altered by a
Q
-transformation.

By
3.3
, we have that
\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle
where
R\in F(a,b)
and
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k+1}}b^{m_{k+1}}
for some
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k\geq 0
. Since
\mathcal{P}
is a regular presentation,
\id:\mathcal{P}\to Q_{4n}
is a group isomorphism and so
R=1
holds in
Q_{4n}
. It follows that
x^{\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}-m_{i}}=1
and so
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}m_{i}\,\,\text{mod}\,2n
.
We can now apply moves as in
Lemma

3.4
(ii) to get that
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}m_{i}
. To see this, let
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}m_{i}+2nr
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. It then takes
|r|
moves to replace
n_{1}
with
n_{1}-nr
and
m_{1}
with
m_{1}+nr
.

We will now show that, given that
\mathcal{P}
presents
Q_{4n}
, we must have
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}m_{i}=\pm 1
. Let
t=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}m_{i}
. We compute the abelianisation

\mathcal{P}^{\ab}\cong_{\gp}\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},x^{2t},[x,y]\rangle\cong_{\gp}\coker(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2t&n\\
0&-2\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\mathbb{Z}^{2})\cong_{\gp}\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2|t|\times\mathbb{Z}/2&\text{if $n$ is even,}\\
\mathbb{Z}/4|t|&\text{if $n$ is odd,}\end{cases}

where
\cong_{\gp}
denotes group isomorphism and the last isomorphism comes from putting the matrix into Smith normal form. It is well known that
Q_{4n}^{\ab}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}
for
n
even, and
Q_{4n}^{\ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}/4
for
n
odd. Hence we have that
|t|=1
and so
t=\pm 1
. In fact, if
t=-1
, then apply inversion as in
Lemma

3.4
(iv) to get
t=1
. Hence we can assume that
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}m_{i}=1
.

Next note that if there exists
i
such that
n_{i}=0
, then we can just write

R=a^{n_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{i-1}}b^{m_{i-1}+m_{i}}a^{n_{i+1}}\cdots b^{m_{k+1}}

and similarly if there exists
i
such that
m_{i}=0
. By induction, this process will end in either (a)
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k+1}}b^{m_{k+1}}
where
k\geq 0
,
n_{i},m_{i}\neq 0
, or (b) one of the cases
R=1
,
R=a^{n_{1}}
for
n_{1}\neq 0
,
R=b^{m_{1}}
for
m_{1}\neq 0
. Suppose for contradiction that case (b) arises. After reduction we have that
R=x^{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. This implies that
Q_{4n}\cong\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},x^{m}\rangle
. Let
d=\gcd(n,m)
. If
d=1
, then there exists
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
an+bm=1
and so
x=(x^{n})^{a}(x^{m})^{b}=y^{2a}
. In particular,
Q_{4n}=\langle y\rangle
is cyclic, which is a contradiction. If
d\neq 1
, then
Q_{4n}/\langle y^{2}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}/d\ast\mathbb{Z}/2
is infinite, which is a contradiction.
Hence the process will terminate in case (a), which is the required presentation.
∎

3.2.
Properties of regular presentations

We are interested in the following two basic tasks:

(1)

Find conditions on
n,k,k^{\prime}
and
n_{i},m_{i},n_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime}
, which determine when

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,n_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime};m_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,m_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}).

(2)

Find conditions on
n_{i},m_{i}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
is a presentation for
Q_{4n}
.

First note that we have the following operations on this class of presentations. This follows directly from the operations listed in
Lemma

3.4
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
k\geq 1
and let
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.

(I)

If
n_{i}\equiv n_{i}^{\prime}\,\,\text{mod}\,n
and
m_{i}\equiv m_{i}^{\prime}\,\,\text{mod}\,n
, then:

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,n_{k}^{\prime};m_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,m_{k}^{\prime}).

(II)

If
n_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
and
m_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
, then:

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{2},\dotsc,n_{k+1};m_{2},\dotsc,m_{k+1}).

(III)

If
n_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
, then:

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k};n_{2},\dotsc,n_{k+1}).

This has the following consequence for presentations of height one, which will be used in the proof of
C
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1};m_{1})
be a presentation of height one. Then:

(i)

There exist
n_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}
with

1\leq n_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}\leq\lceil n/2\rceil
and
\mathcal{P}\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1}^{\prime};m_{1}^{\prime})
.

(ii)

If
n_{1}=1
or
m_{1}=1
, then
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1};m_{1})\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\text{std}}
.

Proof.

(i) By applying (I) we may freely alter the values of
n_{1}
and
m_{1}
mod
n
. We will assume that
1\leq n_{1},m_{1}\leq n-1
. By successively applying operation (III), we get that

\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1};m_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(III)}}}{{\simeq_{Q}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}(m_{1};1-n_{1}+n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(III)}}}{{\simeq_{Q}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}(1-n_{1}+n;1-m_{1}+n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(III)}}}{{\simeq_{Q}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}(1-m_{1}+n;n_{1}).

Note that (III)
{}^{2}=
(II) and so no further presentations can be obtained by applying (II).

If
a\in\mathbb{Z}
with
1\leq a\leq n-1
, then
2\leq 1-a+n\leq n
and
a+(1-a+n)=n+1
implies that
\min\{a,1-a+n\}\leq\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\rfloor=\lceil\frac{n}{2}\rceil
.
In particular, since
1\leq n_{1},m_{1}\leq n-1
, there exists
n_{1}^{\prime}\in\{n_{1},1-n_{1}+n\}
,
m_{1}^{\prime}\in\{m_{1},1-m_{1}+n\}
such that
1\leq n_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}\leq\lceil n/2\rceil
. Hence, by choosing the appropriate number of operations (III), we get that
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1};m_{1})\simeq_{Q}\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1}^{\prime};m_{1}^{\prime})
for some
n_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}
such that
1\leq n_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}\leq\lceil n/2\rceil
.

(ii) This is clear from the fact that, if
n_{1}=1
or
m_{1}=1
, then
n_{2}=0
or
m_{2}=0
. In either case, the relator
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}a^{n_{2}}b^{m_{2}}
can be cyclically permuted to give
ab
.
∎

We now deal with the question of when
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
presents
Q_{4n}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
k\geq 1
and let
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Let
G
denote the group described by
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
. Then:

(i)

There is a group isomorphism
G/\langle xyxy^{-1}\rangle\cong Q_{4n}
where
x\mapsto x
,
y\mapsto y
.

(ii)

G^{\ab}\cong Q_{4n}^{\ab}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
a=x
and
b=yxy^{-1}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle G/\langle ab\rangle

\displaystyle\cong\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},ab,a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k+1}}b^{m_{k+1}}\rangle

\displaystyle\cong\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},ab,a^{\sum n_{i}-\sum m_{i}}\rangle\cong\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},xyxy^{-1}\rangle\cong Q_{4n}.

(ii) This follows from the argument given in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
k\geq 1
and let
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Then if
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
presents
Q_{4n}
then it does so on the standard generating set, i.e.
\id:\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})\to Q_{4n}
,
x\mapsto x
,
y\mapsto y
is a group isomorphism.

Proof.

This follows from
3.7
(i) since it implies that there exists a surjective homomorphism
f:\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})\to Q_{4n}
,
x\mapsto x
,
y\mapsto y
and this map must be a bijection since both groups a finite and have the same order.
∎

The following is a broad generalisation of the example
\mathcal{E}_{n,3}=\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;-2)
of Mannan–Popiel.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
k\geq 0
and let
1\leq n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k}\leq 2n-2
be even integers. Then
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};-n_{k},\dotsc,-n_{1})
is a regular presentation for
Q_{4n}
.

By
Lemma

3.5
, we lose no generality in assuming that
1\leq n_{i}\leq 2n-2
. If
n
is even, then we can assume further that
1\leq n_{i}\leq n-2
.

Proof.

Let
n_{0}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
, let
a=x
and
b=yxy^{-1}
. Let
G
denote the group described by
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};-n_{k},\dotsc,-n_{1})
.
By
3.7
(i), it suffices to show that
ab=1
holds in
G
. Our strategy will be to use regrouping as described in
Lemma

3.4
(v). That is, for any
s_{i},t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\geq 1
, we have that
a^{s_{1}}b^{t_{1}}\cdots a^{s_{m}}b^{t_{m}}=1
implies
b^{s_{1}}a^{t_{1}}\cdots b^{s_{m}}a^{t_{m}}=1
in
G
.

Recall that our presentation is defined by taking
m_{i}=-n_{k-i+1}
for each
1\leq i\leq k
in the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
. Note that
m_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}m_{i}=2-n_{0}
. In
G
, we therefore have that
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{-n_{k}}\cdots a^{n_{k}}b^{-n_{1}}a^{n_{0}}b^{2-n_{0}}=1
.
By regrouping, we get that
b^{n_{1}}a^{-n_{k}}\cdots b^{n_{k}}a^{-n_{1}}b^{n_{0}}a^{2-n_{0}}=1
. Cyclically permuting then gives that

b^{n_{0}}a^{2-n_{0}}b^{n_{1}}a^{-n_{k}}\cdots b^{n_{k}}a^{-n_{1}}=1.

We can now multiply this on the right by
R
to get:

1=(b^{n_{0}}a^{2-n_{0}}b^{n_{1}}a^{-n_{k}}\cdots b^{n_{k}}a^{-n_{1}})\cdot(a^{n_{1}}b^{-n_{k}}\cdots a^{n_{k}}b^{-n_{1}}a^{n_{0}}b^{2-n_{0}})=b^{n_{0}}a^{2}b^{2-n_{0}}

which implies that
a^{2}b^{2}=1
, i.e.
a^{2}=b^{-2}
.

For
1\leq i\leq k
,
n_{i}
is even and so this implies that
a^{n_{i}}=b^{-n_{i}}
. Since
n_{0}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
,
n_{0}
is odd and so
a^{n_{0}-1}=b^{1-n_{0}}
. Substituting this into
R
gives that
a^{2\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}}a^{2n_{0}-1}b=1
which reduces further to
ab=1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Example 3.10
.

Let
k\geq 0
. For each
1\leq i\leq 2k
, let
n_{i}=2
for
i
odd and
n_{i}=-2
for
i
even. This gives the presentations:

\mathcal{P}_{n,k}=\mathcal{P}_{n}(\underbrace{2,-2,\dotsc,2,-2}_{2k};\underbrace{2,-2,\dotsc,2,-2}_{2k})=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},(x^{2}yx^{2}y^{-1}x^{-2}yx^{-2}y^{-1})^{k}xyxy^{-1}\rangle.

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{n,0}=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\text{std}}
is the standard presentation.

Example 3.11
.

Let
k\geq 0
. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, take
n_{i}=2
. This gives the presentations:

\mathcal{P}_{n,k}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{n}(\underbrace{2,\dotsc,2}_{k};\underbrace{-2,\dotsc,-2}_{k})=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},(x^{2}yx^{-2}y^{-1})^{k}x^{1-2k}yx^{1+2k}y^{-1}\rangle.

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{n,0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\text{std}}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{n,1}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;-2)=\mathcal{E}_{n,3}
is the Mannan–Popiel presentation.

We next give conditions under which
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
does not present
Q_{4n}
.
The following will be proven in
Section

3.4
.
The case
\mathcal{E}_{n,r}
was conjectured in
[
MP21
, p2071]
.

Proposition 3.12
.

If
r\equiv 2\,\,\text{mod}\,3
and
3\mid n
, then
\mathcal{E}_{n,r}:=\mathcal{P}_{n}(2;1-r)
and
\mathcal{P}_{n}(r;r)
are not regular presentations for
Q_{4n}
.

Remark 3.13
.

It is possible to use Magma
[
BCP97
]
to determine whether a presentation presents
Q_{4n}
for a specified integer
n\geq 2
. Such computations suggest that the presentations listed above are the only Mannan–Popiel presentations which do not present
Q_{4n}
, i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{n,r}
presents
Q_{4n}
if and only if
r\not\equiv 2\,\,\text{mod}\,3
or
3\nmid n
. We verified this computationally for
n\leq 25
.

We conclude by remarking that we only considered presentations
\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
which present
Q_{4n}
on the standard generating set. It is not clear whether or not presentations of this form exist which do not have this property. However, we can show the following.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle
be a presentation for
Q_{4n}
for some
R\in F(x,y)
. Then at least one of the following holds:

(i)

\id:\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\to Q_{4n}
,
x\mapsto x,y\mapsto y
is a group isomorphism

(ii)

\varphi:\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\to Q_{4n}
,
x\mapsto xy,y\mapsto y
is a group isomorphism.

Furthermore, if
n=2
or
n\not\equiv 2\,\,\text{mod}\,4
, then
(i)
holds, i.e.
\mathcal{P}
is a regular presentation for
Q_{4n}
.

Proof.

We will begin by stating some well-known facts about
Q_{4n}
(see
[
CSST22
, Section 5.8]
,
[
GG04
, Section 1]
). The elements of
Q_{4n}
are
\{x^{i}y^{j}:i\in\mathbb{Z}/2n,j\in\mathbb{Z}/2\}
. For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}/2n
, the elements
x^{i}
have order
\gcd(i,2n)
and the elements
x^{i}y
have order
4
. The centre is
Z(Q_{4n})=\{1,x^{n}\}
.
The generating pairs for
Q_{4n}
are
\{x^{i},x^{j}y\}
and
\{x^{i}y,x^{i+j}y\}
for
i\in(\mathbb{Z}/2n)^{\times}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}/2n
.

For
n=2
, the automorphism group is given by:

\Aut(Q_{8})=\{\theta_{i,j}:x\mapsto x^{i},y\mapsto x^{j}y\}\cup\{\theta_{i,j}^{\prime}:x\mapsto x^{j}y,y\mapsto x^{i}\}\cup\{\theta_{i,j}^{\prime\prime}:x\mapsto x^{j}y,y\mapsto x^{i+j}y\}\cong S_{4}\\

where
i\in\{\pm 1\},j\in\mathbb{Z}/4
in each set. For
n\geq 3
, we have:

\Aut(Q_{4n})=\{\theta_{i,j}:x\mapsto x^{i},y\mapsto x^{j}y:i\in(\mathbb{Z}/2n)^{\times},j\in\mathbb{Z}/2n\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2n\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}/2n)^{\times}.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is a presentation for
Q_{4n}
, there is a group isomorphism
f:\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\to Q_{4n}
.
Let
a=f(x)
and
b=f(y)
. These elements have the property that
a^{n}=y^{2}
and
\langle a,b\rangle=Q_{4n}
.

If
n=2
, then there exists
\theta\in\Aut(Q_{8})
such that
\theta(x)=a
and
\theta(y)=b
. In particular,
\id=\theta^{-1}\circ f:\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R\rangle\to Q_{4n}
is a group isomorphism and so (i) holds.

Now suppose
n\geq 3
. Note that
a^{n}=b^{2}
is non-zero and central since it commutes with
a,b
. This implies that
a^{n}=b^{2}=x^{n}=y^{2}\in Q_{4n}
. We have
b^{4}=1
,
b^{2}\neq 1
and so
b
has order
4
. This implies that either
b=x^{i}
where
\gcd(i,2n)=4
or
b=x^{j}y
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}/2n
. In the first case,
b=x^{i}
cannot be part of a generating pair since
\gcd(i,2n)\neq 1
. Hence we have
b=x^{j}y
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}/2n
. By replacing
f
with
\theta_{1,j}^{-1}\circ f
and relabelling
a
and
b
, we can now assume that
b=y
. It remains to determine
a
which satisfies
a^{n}=x^{n}
and
\langle a,y\rangle=Q_{4n}
. The second condition implies that
a=x^{i}
or
x^{i}y
for some
i\in(\mathbb{Z}/2n)^{\times}
. By replacing
f
with
\theta_{i,0}^{-1}\circ f
and relabelling
a
and
b
, we get that
a=x
or
xy
and
b=y
.
If
a=xy
then we additionally require that
(xy)^{n}=x^{n}
. We have
(xy)^{2}=y^{2}=x^{n}
and so this is equivalent to
(xy)^{n-2}=1
. Since
xy\in Q_{4n}
has order 4, this holds if and only if
n\equiv 2\,\,\text{mod}\,4
. Hence, if
n\not\equiv 2\,\,\text{mod}\,4
, then (i) holds.
∎

3.3.
The second homotopy group of a regular presentation

Recall from
Section

2.2
that, if
G
is a group with free period 4 (of which
Q_{4n}
for
n\geq 2
are examples), then there is a map
\widehat{\Psi}:\HT(G)\to\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/\sim
, where
P\sim Q
if there exist ideals
I,J\leq\mathbb{Z}G
and
n\geq 0
such that
P\cong I\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
,
Q\cong J\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{n}
and
I\cong(N,r)\otimes J_{\theta}
for some
r\in(\mathbb{Z}/|G|)^{\times}
,
\theta\in\Aut(G)
.

From now on, let
n\geq 2
, let
k\geq 0
, let
n_{1},m_{1},\dots,n_{k},m_{k}\neq 0
, and let
R=a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{k+1}}b^{m_{k+1}}
for
n_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
and
m_{k+1}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{R}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},R(x,yxy^{-1})\rangle
, which coincides with
\mathcal{P}_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
defined previously. We will write
G=Q_{4n}
.
Define
\lambda_{R}=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}x^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}-m_{j})}\cdot\partial_{x}(x^{n_{i}})\in\mathbb{Z}[\langle x\rangle]\leq\mathbb{Z}G
where
\partial_{x}
denotes Fox differentiation and

\partial_{x}(x^{r})=\frac{x^{r}-1}{x-1}=\begin{cases}1+x+\dotsb+x^{r-1}&\text{if $r>0$,}\\
0&\text{if $r=0$,}\\
-x^{r}(1+x+\dotsb+x^{|r|-1})&\text{if $r<0$.}\end{cases}

The aim of this section will be to establish the following theorem.

Theorem 3.15
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}_{R}
is a regular presentation for
G
. Then there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}G
such that
\alpha\cdot(xy-1)\in 1+\mathbb{Z}G\cdot\lambda_{R}
and, for any such
\alpha
, we have

\widehat{\Psi}(\mathcal{P}_{R})=[I]\in\SF(\mathbb{Z}G)/\sim

where
I
is the ideal
(1+(1-xy)\alpha,\lambda_{R}\alpha)\leq\mathbb{Z}G
.

Since
xyxy^{-1}=1
implies
R(x,yxy^{-1})=1
, there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}G
for which

(\partial_{x}R(x,yxy^{-1}),\partial_{y}R(x,yxy^{-1}))=\lambda\cdot(\partial_{x}(xyxy^{-1}),\partial_{y}(xyxy^{-1}))=\lambda\cdot(1+xy,x-1)\in\mathbb{Z}G^{2}.

The choice of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}G
is not unique since we can add on multiples of
\lambda_{0}=(\sum_{i=0}^{2n-1}x^{i})\cdot(1-xy)
.

Lemma 3.16
.

We have
(\partial_{x}R(x,yxy^{-1}),\partial_{y}R(x,yxy^{-1}))=\lambda_{R}\cdot(1+xy,x-1)\in\mathbb{Z}G^{2}
. Furthermore,
\lambda_{R}
is the unique element of
\mathbb{Z}[\langle x\rangle]\leq\mathbb{Z}G
with this property.

Proof.

Let
f:F(a,b)\to F(x,y)
,
a\mapsto x
,
b\mapsto yxy^{-1}
. By
2.13
, we have

\partial R(x,yxy^{-1})=\partial f(R(a,b))=f(\partial_{a}R(a,b))\partial f(a)+f(\partial_{b}R(a,b))\partial f(b)

where
\partial=(\partial_{x},\partial_{y})
. We have
\partial f(a)=(1,0)
and
\partial f(b)=(y,1-x^{-1})
.

Since
ab=1
implies
R(a,b)=1
, we have that
\partial ab\mid\partial R(a,b)
in
\mathbb{Z}[F(a,b)/ab]
, where
\partial=(\partial_{a},\partial_{b})
. Since
\partial ab=(1,a)
, it follows that
\partial_{b}R(a,b)=\partial_{a}R(a,b)a\in\mathbb{Z}[F(a,b)/ab]
. Since
f(ab)\mapsto 1\in G
, this implies that
f(\partial_{b}R(a,b))=f(\partial_{a}R(a,b))x\in\mathbb{Z}G
, and so

\partial R(x,yxy^{-1})=f(\partial_{a}R(a,b))\cdot[(1,0)+x(y,1-x^{-1})]=f(\partial_{a}R(a,b))(1+xy,x-1).

By
2.12
, we have

\partial_{a}R(a,b)=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}a^{n_{1}}b^{m_{1}}\cdots a^{n_{i-1}}b^{m_{i-1}}\partial_{a}(a^{n_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}a^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}-m_{j})}\cdot\partial_{a}(a^{n_{i}})\in\mathbb{Z}[F(a,b)/ab]

which implies that
f(\partial_{a}R(a,b))=\lambda_{R}
, as required.

If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[\langle x\rangle]
satisfy this equation, then
\lambda\cdot(1+xy,x-1)\in\mathbb{Z}G^{2}
where
\lambda:=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[\langle x\rangle]
. In particular,
\lambda+(x\lambda)y=0\in\mathbb{Z}G
which implies that
\lambda=0
and so
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
.
∎

We will often write
\lambda_{R}=\lambda_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k})
.
We have

\lambda_{n}(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k};-n_{k},\dotsc,-n_{1})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}+n_{k+1-j})}\sigma(n_{i})-x^{1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}}\sigma(\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}-1)

where, for
r>0
, we let
\sigma(r)=1+x+\dotsb+x^{r-1}
and
\sigma(0)=0
.
For
k=1
and
n_{1}>0
, we have
\lambda_{n}(n_{1};m_{1})=\sigma(n_{1})-x^{1-m_{1}}\sigma(n_{1}-1)
. For example,
\lambda_{n}(2;1-r)=1+x-x^{r}
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}_{R}
is a regular presentation for
G
. Then:

(i)

There exist
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}G
such that
\alpha\cdot(xy-1)+\beta\cdot\lambda_{R}=1
.

(ii)

\lambda_{R}\in\mathbb{Q}G^{\times}
. In particular,
\lambda_{R}\in\mathbb{Z}G
is not a zero divisor.

(iii)

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}G
be any element as in
(i)
. Then there is an injective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module homomorphism

i_{R}:\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}_{R}})\to\mathbb{Z}G/N

whose image is the ideal
(1+(1-xy)\alpha,\lambda_{R}\alpha)\leq\mathbb{Z}G/N
.

Proof.

(i) Consider the standard presentation
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}=\langle x,y\mid x^{n}y^{-2},xyxy^{-1}\rangle
. Let
\partial_{i}
,
\partial_{i}^{\prime}
denote the boundary maps in
C(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}})
and
C(\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{P}_{R}})
respectively. By Fox differentiation, we have

(3)

\partial_{2}=\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1+x+\cdots+x^{n-1}&-(1+y)\\
1+xy&x-1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad\partial_{2}^{\prime}=\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1+x+\cdots+x^{n-1}&-(1+y)\\
\lambda_{R}\cdot(1+xy)&\lambda_{R}\cdot(x-1)\end{smallmatrix}\right).

By
[
CE56
, p253]
, we have that
\ker(\partial_{2})=\mathbb{Z}G\cdot(x-1,1-xy)
.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{R}
is regular,
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\std}
and
\mathcal{P}_{R}
are both presentations for
G
on the same generating set. This implies that
\ker(\partial_{1})=\ker(\partial_{1}^{\prime})
and so, by exactness,
\IM(\partial_{2})=\IM(\partial_{2}^{\prime})
. This implies that there exists
\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}G
such that
(1+xy,x-1)=\partial_{2}^{\prime}(\gamma,\beta)
. Since
\partial_{2}^{\prime}(\gamma,\beta)=\partial_{2}(\gamma,\beta\cdot\lambda_{R})
, this rearranges to
\partial_{2}(\gamma,\beta\cdot\lambda_{R}-1)=0
and so
(\gamma,\beta\cdot\lambda_{R}-1)\in\ker(\partial_{2})
. Thus there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}G
such that
(\gamma,\beta\cdot\lambda_{R}-1)=\alpha\cdot(x-1,1-xy)
. In particular,
\alpha\cdot(xy-1)+\beta\cdot\lambda_{R}=1
and
\gamma=\alpha\cdot(x-1)
.

(ii) By
Lemma

3.16
, we have that
\lambda_{R}\in\mathbb{Z}C_{2n}\leq\mathbb{Q}C_{2n}
where
C_{2n}=\langle x\rangle\leq Q_{4n}
.
There is an isomorphism of rings
\mathbb{Q}C_{2n}\cong\prod_{d\mid 2n}\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{d})
where
\zeta_{d}
denotes a primitive
d
th root of unity. Let
f_{d}:\mathbb{Q}C_{2n}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{d})
denote the map induced by
x\mapsto\zeta_{d}
. Since
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{d})^{\times}=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{d})\setminus\{0\}
, we obtain

\mathbb{Q}C_{2n}^{\times}=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}C_{2n}:f_{d}(\alpha)\neq 0\text{ for all $d\mid 2n$}\}.

It therefore remains to show that
f_{d}(\lambda_{R})\neq 0
for all
d\mid 2n
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d},y]=(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d}])\{1,y\}
denote the cyclic algebra such that
y^{2}=1
if
d\mid n
and
y^{2}=-1
otherwise.
Then there is an isomorphism of rings
\mathbb{Q}G\cong\prod_{d\mid 2n}\mathbb{Q}[\zeta_{d},y]
where
\mathbb{Q}[\zeta_{d},y]=\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d},y]

[
Swa83
, p75]
.
Let
F_{d}:\mathbb{Q}G\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Q}[\zeta_{d},y]
denote the map
x\mapsto\zeta_{d}
,
y\mapsto y
and note that
F_{d}|_{\mathbb{Q}C_{2n}}=f_{d}
and
F_{d}(\mathbb{Z}G)=\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d},j]
.

Suppose for contradiction that
f_{d}(\lambda_{R})=0
for some
d\mid 2n
. By the proof of (i), we have that
(1+xy,x-1)=\partial_{2}^{\prime}(\gamma,\beta)
.
Applying
F_{d}
to this equation gives:

(4)

(1+\zeta_{d}y,\zeta_{d}-1)=F_{d}(\gamma)\cdot(1+\zeta_{d}+\cdots+\zeta_{d}^{n-1},-(1+y))\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d},y]^{2}

where, since
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}G
, we have
F_{d}(\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d},y]
. The second coordinate gives that
\zeta_{d}-1=\delta(1+y)
for some
\delta\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d},y]
. If
\delta=a+by
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d}]
, this implies that
\zeta_{d}-1=(a\pm b)+(a+b)y

where the sign
\pm
depends on whether or not
d\mid n
. Comparing coefficients of
y
gives that
b=-a
and so
\zeta_{d}-1=a\pm(-a)
. If
d\mid n
, this gives that
\zeta_{d}-1=0
which is a contradiction. If
d\nmid n
, this gives that
\zeta_{d}-1=2a
and so
\frac{1}{2}(\zeta_{d}-1)\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{d}]
which is a contradiction. Hence
f_{d}(\lambda_{R})\neq 0
, which completes the proof.
Note that, in the case
d\mid n
, we could have immediately obtained a contradiction from the first coordinate of
Eq.

4
since
1+\zeta_{d}+\cdots+\zeta_{d}^{n-1}=0
and
1+\zeta_{d}y\neq 0
.

(iii) Recall that
\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}_{R}})=\ker(\partial_{2}^{\prime})
. Define
f:\ker(\partial_{2}^{\prime})\to\ker(\partial_{2})
,
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(x_{1},x_{2}\lambda_{R})
.
By (ii),
\lambda_{R}
is not a zero divisor and so
f
is injective.
By
[
CE56
, p253]
, there is an isomorphism
g:\mathbb{Z}G/N\to\ker(\partial_{2})
,
1\mapsto(x-1,1-xy)
. We now define
i_{R}
to be the composition

i_{R}:\pi_{2}(X_{\mathcal{P}_{R}})=\ker(\partial_{2}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{f}\ker(\partial_{2})\xrightarrow{g^{-1}}\mathbb{Z}G/N

which is injective since
f
and
g^{-1}
are injective.

It follows directly from the definition of
\gamma
,
\beta
given in (i) that
\ker(\partial_{2}^{\prime})=\mathbb{Z}G\cdot A+\mathbb{Z}G\cdot B
where

(5)

A=[(x-1)+(1-xy)\gamma,(1-xy)\beta],\quad B=[-\lambda_{R}\gamma,1-\lambda_{R}\beta].

The equivalent statement for right modules is established in
[
MP21
, Lemma 6.2]
. By substituting
\gamma=\alpha\cdot(x-1)
and
\beta\lambda_{R}=1-\alpha(1-xy)
, we obtain

\displaystyle f(A)

\displaystyle=[(1+(1-xy)\alpha)(x-1),(1-xy)\beta\lambda_{R}]=(1+(1-xy)\alpha)\cdot[x-1,1-xy]

\displaystyle f(B)
